**Proverbs 21**
*The king's heart is in the hand of the Lord, as the rivers of water: he turneth it whithersoever he will.*

*2 Every way of a man is right in his own eyes: but the Lord pondereth the hearts.*

*3 To do justice and judgment is more acceptable to the Lord than sacrifice.*

*4 An high look, and a proud heart, and the plowing of the wicked, is sin.*

*5 The thoughts of the diligent tend only to plenteousness; but of every one that is hasty only to want.*

*6 The getting of treasures by a lying tongue is a vanity tossed to and fro of them that seek death.*

*7 The robbery of the wicked shall destroy them; because they refuse to do judgment.*

*8 The way of man is froward and strange: but as for the pure, his work is right.*

*9 It is better to dwell in a corner of the housetop, than with a brawling woman in a wide house.*

*10 The soul of the wicked desireth evil: his neighbour findeth no favour in his eyes.*

*11 When the scorner is punished, the simple is made wise: and when the wise is instructed, he receiveth knowledge.*

*12 The righteous man wisely considereth the house of the wicked: but God overthroweth the wicked for their wickedness.*

*13 Whoso stoppeth his ears at the cry of the poor, he also shall cry himself, but shall not be heard.*

*14 A gift in secret pacifieth anger: and a reward in the bosom strong wrath.*

*15 It is joy to the just to do judgment: but destruction shall be to the workers of iniquity.*

*16 The man that wandereth out of the way of understanding shall remain in the congregation of the dead.*

*17 He that loveth pleasure shall be a poor man: he that loveth wine and oil shall not be rich.*

*18 The wicked shall be a ransom for the righteous, and the transgressor for the upright.*

*19 It is better to dwell in the wilderness, than with a contentious and an angry woman.*

*20 There is treasure to be desired and oil in the dwelling of the wise; but a foolish man spendeth it up.*

*21 He that followeth after righteousness and mercy findeth life, righteousness, and honour.*

*22 A wise man scaleth the city of the mighty, and casteth down the strength of the confidence thereof.*

*23 Whoso keepeth his mouth and his tongue keepeth his soul from troubles.*

*24 Proud and haughty scorner is his name, who dealeth in proud wrath.*

*25 The desire of the slothful killeth him; for his hands refuse to labour.*

*26 He coveteth greedily all the day long: but the righteous giveth and spareth not.*

*27 The sacrifice of the wicked is abomination: how much more, when he bringeth it with a wicked mind?*

*28 A false witness shall perish: but the man that heareth speaketh constantly.*

*29 A wicked man hardeneth his face: but as for the upright, he directeth his way.*

*30 There is no wisdom nor understanding nor counsel against the Lord.*

*31 The horse is prepared against the day of battle: but safety is of the Lord.*

## Proverbs 22  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-09

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17264

<!-- post 17264 · 2026-03-09 05:30 · scripture -->

**Proverbs 22**
*A good name is rather to be chosen than great riches, and loving favour rather than silver and gold.*

*2 The rich and poor meet together: the Lord is the maker of them all.*

*3 A prudent man foreseeth the evil, and hideth himself: but the simple pass on, and are punished.*

*4 By humility and the fear of the Lord are riches, and honour, and life.*

*5 Thorns and snares are in the way of the froward: he that doth keep his soul shall be far from them.*

*6 Train up a child in the way he should go: and when he is old, he will not depart from it.*

*7 The rich ruleth over the poor, and the borrower is servant to the lender.*

*8 He that soweth iniquity shall reap vanity: and the rod of his anger shall fail.*

*9 He that hath a bountiful eye shall be blessed; for he giveth of his bread to the poor.*

*10 Cast out the scorner, and contention shall go out; yea, strife and reproach shall cease.*

*11 He that loveth pureness of heart, for the grace of his lips the king shall be his friend.*

*12 The eyes of the Lord preserve knowledge, and he overthroweth the words of the transgressor.*

*13 The slothful man saith, There is a lion without, I shall be slain in the streets.*

*14 The mouth of strange women is a deep pit: he that is abhorred of the Lord shall fall therein.*

*15 Foolishness is bound in the heart of a child; but the rod of correction shall drive it far from him.*

*16 He that oppresseth the poor to increase his riches, and he that giveth to the rich, shall surely come to want.*

*17 Bow down thine ear, and hear the words of the wise, and apply thine heart unto my knowledge.*

*18 For it is a pleasant thing if thou keep them within thee; they shall withal be fitted in thy lips.*

*19 That thy trust may be in the Lord, I have made known to thee this day, even to thee.*

*20 Have not I written to thee excellent things in counsels and knowledge,*

*21 That I might make thee know the certainty of the words of truth; that thou mightest answer the words of truth to them that send unto thee?*

*22 Rob not the poor, because he is poor: neither oppress the afflicted in the gate:*

*23 For the Lord will plead their cause, and spoil the soul of those that spoiled them.*

*24 Make no friendship with an angry man; and with a furious man thou shalt not go:*

*25 Lest thou learn his ways, and get a snare to thy soul.*

*26 Be not thou one of them that strike hands, or of them that are sureties for debts.*

*27 If thou hast nothing to pay, why should he take away thy bed from under thee?*

*28 Remove not the ancient landmark, which thy fathers have set.*

*29 Seest thou a man diligent in his business? he shall stand before kings; he shall not stand before mean men.*

<!-- post 17265 · 2026-03-09 05:56 · study -->

The wisdom given Solomon, which he passed on to his son, continues to drive home the lessons of life. The inspired word, *profitable for doctrine, for reproof, for correction, for instruction in righteousness: That the man of God may be perfect, throughly furnished unto all good works.*

A good name, being known as upright and honest, is more valuable than the riches life has to offer. 

The rich and the poor meet together in death. The Lord is the maker of them all. This perspective is how God sees it, and why He is not a respecter of persons. Why a good name is preferable to gold and silver. Why walking in the way of the Lord is wise, regardless of your station. From the soon to be king, Rheaboham to the least in the kingdom. 

A prudent man sees evil coming and hides himself from it, while the simple walk right into it. In the same application of having a good name over riches and knowing the like end of all men, knowing the evil that awaits the wicked, prudence requires making peace with God in the way, before the judgment. 

**Matthew 5:25-26** 
*Agree with thine adversary quickly, whiles thou art in the way with him; lest at any time the adversary deliver thee to the judge, and the judge deliver thee to the officer, and thou be cast into prison. Verily I say unto thee, Thou shalt by no means come out thence, till thou hast paid the uttermost farthing.*

Settle your account with God now, by humility and fear of Him, and you will see riches and honor and life. Better than at the judgment which awaits all, great and small. Rich and poor. Hide yourself from evil, in Christ. 

Don't be caught in the thorns and snares in the way of the froward. Their path leads to death. Keep your soul far from their ways. 

*Train up a child in the way he should go: and when he is old, he will not depart from it.* The wisdom of raising Godly children, that they have a healthy fear of the Lord, understanding of what's right, isn't a guarantee of their being righteous in their adulthood, but it certainly is a great inoculation against the snares and stumbling stones most people get caught on. 

Christian parents often neglect this, and hope Godliness will be passed on by osmosis from one church service a week. By a few hours a week with a youth group. 

Then they panic about what will happen when their child goes off into the world, and face heartbreak when they stray far from the way of God. 

Well meaning parents who think they are doing well for their child neglect wisdom, like; *Foolishness is bound in the heart of a child; but the rod of correction shall drive it far from him.*

Solomon has repeatedly coupled the training up of a child in wisdom and the rod of correction driving foolishness from them, because it is this balance that produces good children. 

**Ephesians 6:4**
*And, ye fathers, provoke not your children to wrath: but bring them up in the nurture and admonition of the Lord.*

Note that the Bible does not count spanking a child as "provoking" a child to wrath. Being an unjust and unmerciful ruler of your house does that. Letting them run wild does that.

Because? *He that soweth iniquity shall reap vanity: and the rod of his anger shall fail.*

<!-- post 17269 · 2026-03-10 05:44 · study -->

Continuing in **Proverbs 22**; the rich rule over the poor, and a borrower serves the lender. This is a truth many struggle with, especially in America today. We complain about the "unfair" reality of billionaires having undue influence and the power of usury, while we rack up debt. While we ignore the wisdom of God. Men diligent in business will stand before kings, after all. 

We ignore the warnings against being slothful, making excuses as foolish as "there are lions without!" to avoid hard work. 

We ignore the calls to be generous givers. *He that hath a bountiful eye shall be blessed; for he giveth of his bread to the poor.* 

We tend to oppress the poor while giving to the rich. Not the state welfare programs and taxes, but the general giving. We will contribute to the rich for things we don't need, while neglecting the brother at church who is struggling to pay for basic necessities through no fault of his own. Our national priorities are as upsidedown as our debt to income ratio. We surely come to want for our foolishness. 

Rob not the poor, yet there are constant perverse incentives designed to do just that, used by the powerful, who lack all mercy and discernment. 

We elevate the scorners, rather than cast them out. We make friends with the angry men who, in their ranting and complaining gain influence and an audience, learning their ways, and becoming bitter ourselves.

We strike hands and become surety for the debts of others, by the same system that refuses to hear the pleas of the afflicted. Our government, by the people, takes away the charitable heart and cheerful giver, by it's taking to feed the sluggard, reward the harlot, give to the rich, and neglect the widows indeed. We take the bed of the poor in their debts, while forgiving the thief and robber, theirs. 

These snares to our soul keep us from the way of God. We have removed all the landmarks of the old paths, and wonder why things are so wicked in our day. 

Landmarks that set boundaries are moved by the covetous and the unrighteous, to obscure their theft, and done to mislead those seeking the truth. They convinced many it's ok to do, to "keep up with the times," or to appeal to "modernism." But these are just excuses to abandon the long-standing truths our fathers stood on.

<!-- post 17270 · 2026-03-10 05:56 · study -->

Chapter 22 begins a transition from the shorter sayings of Solomon to what others have labeled the "words to the wise." The transition is marked by a clear summary of the purpose of the entire collection. 

*Bow down thine ear, and hear the words of the wise, and apply thine heart unto my knowledge. For it is a pleasant thing if thou keep them within thee; they shall withal be fitted in thy lips. That thy trust may be in the Lord, I have made known to thee this day, even to thee. Have not I written to thee excellent things in counsels and knowledge, That I might make thee know the certainty of the words of truth; that thou mightest answer the words of truth to them that send unto thee?*

This appeal to Rehoboam by his loving father is an inspired echo of God's appeal to His children, to learn the more excellent way, that it be well with us. Excellent counsel that we can lean on, learn and do. So incline your ear. Ponder these things. Put them into practice. Put scorning, foolishness, iniquity, evil, a froward mouth, slothfulness, and all other wickedness far from you. Let God's wisdom fill you, and guide your life in all things. Business, marriage, raising children, dealing with neighbors and strangers and temptations, wrath and envy. 

In all thy ways acknowledging God, that He may direct our paths.

## Proverbs 23  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-11

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17272

<!-- post 17272 · 2026-03-11 06:41 · scripture -->

**Proverbs 23**
*When thou sittest to eat with a ruler, consider diligently what is before thee:*

*2 And put a knife to thy throat, if thou be a man given to appetite.*

*3 Be not desirous of his dainties: for they are deceitful meat.*

*4 Labour not to be rich: cease from thine own wisdom.*

*5 Wilt thou set thine eyes upon that which is not? for riches certainly make themselves wings; they fly away as an eagle toward heaven.*

*6 Eat thou not the bread of him that hath an evil eye, neither desire thou his dainty meats:*

*7 For as he thinketh in his heart, so is he: Eat and drink, saith he to thee; but his heart is not with thee.*

*8 The morsel which thou hast eaten shalt thou vomit up, and lose thy sweet words.*

*9 Speak not in the ears of a fool: for he will despise the wisdom of thy words.*

*10 Remove not the old landmark; and enter not into the fields of the fatherless:*

*11 For their redeemer is mighty; he shall plead their cause with thee.*

*12 Apply thine heart unto instruction, and thine ears to the words of knowledge.*

*13 Withhold not correction from the child: for if thou beatest him with the rod, he shall not die.*

*14 Thou shalt beat him with the rod, and shalt deliver his soul from hell.*

*15 My son, if thine heart be wise, my heart shall rejoice, even mine.*

*16 Yea, my reins shall rejoice, when thy lips speak right things.*

*17 Let not thine heart envy sinners: but be thou in the fear of the Lord all the day long.*

*18 For surely there is an end; and thine expectation shall not be cut off.*

*19 Hear thou, my son, and be wise, and guide thine heart in the way.*

*20 Be not among winebibbers; among riotous eaters of flesh:*

*21 For the drunkard and the glutton shall come to poverty: and drowsiness shall clothe a man with rags.*

*22 Hearken unto thy father that begat thee, and despise not thy mother when she is old.*

*23 Buy the truth, and sell it not; also wisdom, and instruction, and understanding.*

*24 The father of the righteous shall greatly rejoice: and he that begetteth a wise child shall have joy of him.*

*25 Thy father and thy mother shall be glad, and she that bare thee shall rejoice.*

*26 My son, give me thine heart, and let thine eyes observe my ways.*

*27 For a whore is a deep ditch; and a strange woman is a narrow pit.*

*28 She also lieth in wait as for a prey, and increaseth the transgressors among men.*

*29 Who hath woe? who hath sorrow? who hath contentions? who hath babbling? who hath wounds without cause? who hath redness of eyes?*

*30 They that tarry long at the wine; they that go to seek mixed wine.*

*31 Look not thou upon the wine when it is red, when it giveth his colour in the cup, when it moveth itself aright.*

*32 At the last it biteth like a serpent, and stingeth like an adder.*

*33 Thine eyes shall behold strange women, and thine heart shall utter perverse things.*

*34 Yea, thou shalt be as he that lieth down in the midst of the sea, or as he that lieth upon the top of a mast.*

*35 They have stricken me, shalt thou say, and I was not sick; they have beaten me, and I felt it not: when shall I awake? I will seek it yet again.*

<!-- post 17273 · 2026-03-11 07:00 · study -->

**Proverbs 23** enters fully into the "words of wisdom" portion of Solomon's sayings, where he provides more fleshed out appeals to his son to walk the paths of wisdom and to avoid folly. 

He begins with a warning about being seduced by the dainties of meat, sumptuous food prepared to curry the prince's favor. Better to put a knife to your throat than allow your own appetite to get the better of you. 

While there is an application here about gluttony and the appeal of food and feast as a means of a ruler or a rich man to gain sway over someone, it can be applied to us, spiritually. 

An important perspective for a saved believer to retain in their mind is that they are both servants of God, and given a ministry of reconciliation towards the lost by Him, and simultaneously, being princes and princesses of the Kingdom, heirs with Christ. 

We are to judge angels, the Bible says. We are going to rule and reign with Christ. These sayings are preparation for Rehoboam, but they have spiritual application for us as well. 

And so the lust of the eyes and pride of life that can pervert judgment, and lead us off the path should be avoided. The deceitful meat of the prince and power of the air, Satan, attacks us through those appetites. Be not desirous of them. Keep them in check. 

In a like manner, working to be rich, putting greed over wisdom, is a fool's errand. Riches are fleeting. Especially those gained through greed. They *fly away as an eagle towards heaven.* Again, lay up treasure in Heaven, not here on Earth. God will provide what we need if we walk in His paths. 

So, beware the temptations given by a man whose heart isn't with you. Who is just looking to manipulate you by your vices, just as Satan does with us. 

The temptations we fail, the pursuit of food, riches, acceptance by the powerful...strange women, it all will lead to our own undoing. *The morsel which thou hast eaten shalt thou vomit up, and lose thy sweet words.*

And if you do, remember not to be as a fool who returns to his folly like a dog returns to his vomit. Learn from your falling, and rise again.

<!-- post 17274 · 2026-03-11 07:08 · study -->

Next, we are told to not speak wisdom to the fool, for they despise it. They do not value wise words, or that which is holy;

**Matthew 7:6**
*Give not that which is holy unto the dogs, neither cast ye your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet, and turn again and rend you.*

Jesus' words against giving the word of God to dogs and swine, the sodomites and the reprobate, is an escalation to the proverb. Just as we will see, arguing with fools and the wicked is vanity in later chapters. It is important to keep in mind, especially today with the Internet culture, where endless debate and the need to prove someone is "wrong on the Internet" can become a hazard. A snare I struggle with. 

**Titus 3:10**
*A man that is an heretick after the first and second admonition reject.*..

As Jesus instructs the Apostles, shake the dust. When we are out soul-winning, going door to door with the gospel, never get into arguments with the unreceptive or the mockers or blasphemers. You simply let them oppose themselves and move on. 

Remembering that the foolish despise wisdom is a great way to train yourself to not be trapped in debate, but to say your piece and move on. Their own blood is on their own head. Wash your hands of it.

<!-- post 17275 · 2026-03-11 08:18 · study -->

Solomon, as in the previous chapter, warns against moving landmarks. Here, in a very literal sense, taking land from the fatherless, stealing the inheritance of those who have no one to stand for their cause. This evil will be rewarded by God, He shall plead their cause, and it won't go well for you. Their Redeemer is mighty, indeed. 

It is reminiscent of the story of Naboth the Jezreelite and his vineyard, told in **1 Kings 21**. Ahab king of Samaria, what was once the Northern Kingdom of Israel, desired the man's land. The queen, Jezebel, conspired to steal the land by false witnesses against Naboth, killing the poor man. Taking his inheritance. 

Following the murder, God sends Elijah to pronounce judgment, prophesying that dogs will lick Ahab's blood where they licked Naboth's, and that Jezebel will be likewise devoured.

God ensured that Ahab met his end at Jezreel, and the blood of Naboth was repaid in kind. 

The perversion of justice is iniquity. The fatherless have an advocate in the Father. Never think those who we seem to have the upperhand over can be taken advantage of without recompense. Their Redeemer liveth.

<!-- post 17276 · 2026-03-12 05:52 · study -->

**Proverbs 23** reinforces the idea of the wise son being a blessing and a cause for rejoicing in parents. The natural need for a child to want to please their parents, to follow in their ways and to be their pride and joy, often gives way to rebellion and a need to establish themselves separately from their parents expectations. 

This is why the early admonition, the scourging betimes, the willingness to both instruct your child in the way of righteousness and beat them with the rod of correction early, will help them have the foundation for fear of the Lord, and an understanding that they shall not depart from. 

The two methods achieve one result. A child isn't going to die from a spanking. The purple of a bruise is a sore reminder of their foolishness and the understanding that there are consequences for their actions. The Godly instruction shows the correct path forward. All of it is given in love. 

When the result is a child with a wise heart and lips speaking the right things, who harkens to the instruction of their youth, it is a cause to rejoice. 

They need to hear about the ends of sin and the wages of death it brings. To see the end of the fool. To rightly judge how the latter end of the sinner is compared to the wise. They should not envy the sinners but fear the Lord. 

Solomon continues the words to the wise; be no among winebibbers or gluttons. Drunks and the overindulgent come to poverty. There are examples in our own families and opportunities to show the results in our streets. 

Many teach the mercy of charity and not judging to their children, without using the wisdom of example, rightly judging the situation. They end up learning a toxic empathy without seeing the fault of the fool leading to their plight. 

A child raised in the fear and admonition of the Lord will seek truth and instruction over riches. 

The warning against whores and strange women especially resonates, as the fairer sex was Solomon's weakness. 

It is important for a young man or woman to hear the truth, even if it is your own sin that you struggle with. Objective truth should be the goal, not hiding or excusing your failings. One of the triggers of rebellion, especially in a Christian home is the dawning realization of hypocrisy from the strict parents, who don't hold themselves to the standard they are imposing. Resentment can take root. 

Honesty about sin and consequences, how it has affected you personally, can help soften the blow when they reach the age where children realize Mom and Dad aren't perfect. And instruction of the gospel lets them know there is One who is perfect and holy, even if there are none righteous among men. 

The final passage paints the dark reality of alcohol. It leads to sorrow and woe. Babblings and wounds without cause, from drunken stumbling. Red eyes and hangovers. 

God's word says not to even look at wine as it ferments, bubbling and turning red. 

So many excuse their drunkenness by using examples of the use of the word "wine" in scripture, but neglect the meaning and context. Wine often mean the fresh juice of the vine, new. Of preserved concentrate  or watered down to the point of being alcohol free. They will say that a little won't hurt...But the standard for princes is to not even look at strong wine. 

The warning that alcohol causes perverse and lustful thoughts in the heart, lowering inhibitions and standards, leading to greater sin, is an undeniable truth. 

The poison of a snake, the toxic nature of the drink, like the bite of an adder leads to disorientation, head spinning, unsteady feet and sickness. Hangovers. And the sad reality that those given to wine will seek it again, despite all those negatives. 

Better to be sober and wise.

<!-- post 17278 · 2026-03-12 19:08 · study -->

The shift in perspective throughout your life really guides how you look at scripture like proverbs. At any given time you can be any one of the people in each of the sayings. The father, or son, the fool or the wise man. The rich or the poor. And all under one Lord.

**Proverbs 23**
*When thou sittest to eat with a ruler, consider diligently what is before thee:*

*2 And put a knife to thy throat, if thou be a man given to appetite.*

*3 Be not desirous of his dainties: for they are deceitful meat.*

*4 Labour not to be rich: cease from thine own wisdom.*

*5 Wilt thou set thine eyes upon that which is not? for riches certainly make themselves wings; they fly away as an eagle toward heaven.*

*6 Eat thou not the bread of him that hath an evil eye, neither desire thou his dainty meats:*

*7 For as he thinketh in his heart, so is he: Eat and drink, saith he to thee; but his heart is not with thee.*

*8 The morsel which thou hast eaten shalt thou vomit up, and lose thy sweet words.*

*9 Speak not in the ears of a fool: for he will despise the wisdom of thy words.*

*10 Remove not the old landmark; and enter not into the fields of the fatherless:*

*11 For their redeemer is mighty; he shall plead their cause with thee.*

*12 Apply thine heart unto instruction, and thine ears to the words of knowledge.*

*13 Withhold not correction from the child: for if thou beatest him with the rod, he shall not die.*

*14 Thou shalt beat him with the rod, and shalt deliver his soul from hell.*

*15 My son, if thine heart be wise, my heart shall rejoice, even mine.*

*16 Yea, my reins shall rejoice, when thy lips speak right things.*

*17 Let not thine heart envy sinners: but be thou in the fear of the Lord all the day long.*

*18 For surely there is an end; and thine expectation shall not be cut off.*

*19 Hear thou, my son, and be wise, and guide thine heart in the way.*

*20 Be not among winebibbers; among riotous eaters of flesh:*

*21 For the drunkard and the glutton shall come to poverty: and drowsiness shall clothe a man with rags.*

*22 Hearken unto thy father that begat thee, and despise not thy mother when she is old.*

*23 Buy the truth, and sell it not; also wisdom, and instruction, and understanding.*

*24 The father of the righteous shall greatly rejoice: and he that begetteth a wise child shall have joy of him.*

*25 Thy father and thy mother shall be glad, and she that bare thee shall rejoice.*

*26 My son, give me thine heart, and let thine eyes observe my ways.*

*27 For a whore is a deep ditch; and a strange woman is a narrow pit.*

*28 She also lieth in wait as for a prey, and increaseth the transgressors among men.*

*29 Who hath woe? who hath sorrow? who hath contentions? who hath babbling? who hath wounds without cause? who hath redness of eyes?*

*30 They that tarry long at the wine; they that go to seek mixed wine.*

*31 Look not thou upon the wine when it is red, when it giveth his colour in the cup, when it moveth itself aright.*

*32 At the last it biteth like a serpent, and stingeth like an adder.*

*33 Thine eyes shall behold strange women, and thine heart shall utter perverse things.*

*34 Yea, thou shalt be as he that lieth down in the midst of the sea, or as he that lieth upon the top of a mast.*

*35 They have stricken me, shalt thou say, and I was not sick; they have beaten me, and I felt it not: when shall I awake? I will seek it yet again.*

## Proverbs 24  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-13

**Proverbs 24**
*Be not thou envious against evil men, neither desire to be with them.*

*2 For their heart studieth destruction, and their lips talk of mischief.*

*3 Through wisdom is an house builded; and by understanding it is established:*

*4 And by knowledge shall the chambers be filled with all precious and pleasant riches.*

*5 A wise man is strong; yea, a man of knowledge increaseth strength.*

*6 For by wise counsel thou shalt make thy war: and in multitude of counsellors there is safety.*

*7 Wisdom is too high for a fool: he openeth not his mouth in the gate.*

*8 He that deviseth to do evil shall be called a mischievous person.*

*9 The thought of foolishness is sin: and the scorner is an abomination to men.*

*10 If thou faint in the day of adversity, thy strength is small.*

*11 If thou forbear to deliver them that are drawn unto death, and those that are ready to be slain;*

*12 If thou sayest, Behold, we knew it not; doth not he that pondereth the heart consider it? and he that keepeth thy soul, doth not he know it? and shall not he render to every man according to his works?*

*13 My son, eat thou honey, because it is good; and the honeycomb, which is sweet to thy taste:*

*14 So shall the knowledge of wisdom be unto thy soul: when thou hast found it, then there shall be a reward, and thy expectation shall not be cut off.*

*15 Lay not wait, O wicked man, against the dwelling of the righteous; spoil not his resting place:*

*16 For a just man falleth seven times, and riseth up again: but the wicked shall fall into mischief.*

*17 Rejoice not when thine enemy falleth, and let not thine heart be glad when he stumbleth:*

*18 Lest the Lord see it, and it displease him, and he turn away his wrath from him.*

*19 Fret not thyself because of evil men, neither be thou envious at the wicked:*

*20 For there shall be no reward to the evil man; the candle of the wicked shall be put out.*

*21 My son, fear thou the Lord and the king: and meddle not with them that are given to change:*

*22 For their calamity shall rise suddenly; and who knoweth the ruin of them both?*

*23 These things also belong to the wise. It is not good to have respect of persons in judgment.*

*24 He that saith unto the wicked, Thou are righteous; him shall the people curse, nations shall abhor him:*

*25 But to them that rebuke him shall be delight, and a good blessing shall come upon them.*

*26 Every man shall kiss his lips that giveth a right answer.*

*27 Prepare thy work without, and make it fit for thyself in the field; and afterwards build thine house.*

*28 Be not a witness against thy neighbour without cause; and deceive not with thy lips.*

*29 Say not, I will do so to him as he hath done to me: I will render to the man according to his work.*

*30 I went by the field of the slothful, and by the vineyard of the man void of understanding;*

*31 And, lo, it was all grown over with thorns, and nettles had covered the face thereof, and the stone wall thereof was broken down.*

*32 Then I saw, and considered it well: I looked upon it, and received instruction.*

*33 Yet a little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to sleep:*

*34 So shall thy poverty come as one that travelleth; and thy want as an armed man.*

<!-- post 17281 · 2026-03-13 06:56 · study -->

Deep in the meat of the word, the heart of these sayings, we really can capture the themes that have been being hammered, again and again, and see the Gospel of hope that is in Proverbs. 

To me, these later sections seem almost to be a New Testament epistle, and much of it would fit there. 

The Epistles of Paul highlight the implications of salvation on the Christian life. Solomon's wisdom does much the same thing. 

**2 Corinthians 6:14**
*Be ye not unequally yoked together with unbelievers: for what fellowship hath righteousness with unrighteousness? and what communion hath light with darkness?*

Be not envious of evil men, or desire to be with them. Don't take part in another man's sin. 

**1 Timothy 5:22**
*Lay hands suddenly on no man, neither be partaker of other men's sins: keep thyself pure.*

**1 Corinthians 5:11** 
*But now I have written unto you not to keep company, if any man that is called a brother be a fornicator, or covetous, or an idolater, or a railer, or a drunkard, or an extortioner; with such an one no not to eat.*

**Ephesians 5** is full of such wisdom; 

*Let no man deceive you with vain words: for because of these things cometh the wrath of God upon the children of disobedience.Be not ye therefore partakers with them*...

*And have no fellowship with the unfruitful works of darkness, but rather reprove them.*..

*See then that ye walk circumspectly, not as fools, but as wise, Redeeming the time, because the days are evil. Wherefore be ye not unwise, but understanding what the will of the Lord is. And be not drunk with wine, wherein is excess; but be filled with the Spirit*...

*Nevertheless let every one of you in particular so love his wife even as himself; and the wife see that she reverence her husband.*

The wisdom of God is clear and consistent throughout the Bible. We are given parable, story, sayings that all convey a single, unified message. The way of God. His ways are not our ways, yet we may know Him, because He has shown us in His word. What a blessing.

<!-- post 17282 · 2026-03-14 08:45 · study -->

Continuing in **Proverbs 24**, we seek the knowledge that is a treasure, given freely by God, and we seek the strength that increased knowledge brings. 

We should seek wise counsel in war, and the safety of a multitude of councillors. It isn't wise to leave things to a committee, but rather, as a leader, seek options and opinions from other wise men. 

Ironically, for Solomon's son, Rehoboam *when he went to Shechem: for all Israel were come to Shechem to make him king*, Jeroboam the son of Nebat, exiled to Egypt, returns to challenge his anointing. 

**1 Kings 12:3-6**
*That they sent and called him. And Jeroboam and all the congregation of Israel came, and spake unto Rehoboam, saying, Thy father made our yoke grievous: now therefore make thou the grievous service of thy father, and his heavy yoke which he put upon us, lighter, and we will serve thee. And he said unto them, Depart yet for three days, then come again to me. And the people departed. And king Rehoboam consulted with the old men, that stood before Solomon his father while he yet lived, and said, How do ye advise that I may answer this people?*

He wisely sought counsel in this crisis. He hears out the old men, the hoary heads who the wisest man ever to live relied on. The very men Solomon was referencing throughout Proverbs. However, the young king sought others, a multitude of counsel, but lacked the discernment to ignore what his untrustworthy heart told him seemed right, and listen to the older men. Verse 8;

*But he forsook the counsel of the old men, which they had given him, and consulted with the young men that were grown up with him, and which stood before him.* 

Had he kept all the wisdom of his father, applying all the sayings he received, the splitting of the kingdom may have been avoided. 

Jeroboam devised evil. He was a mischievous person. He was a scorner, leading false (or exaggerated) charges against the deceased king, winning the fools with lies in an evil cause. And so Rehoboam fainted in the day of adversity. His strength was small. And a kingdom was lost, along with the sealing the ultimate end of the northern 10 tribes.

<!-- post 17283 · 2026-03-14 09:04 · study -->

While it is rare, there are times when you are out soul-winning, or giving the gospel to others, where the person refuses to admit that they sin. Recognizing one has fallen short of the glory of God is a key step on the Roman's Road. Sometimes, it's pride, other times it is ignorance, but regardless of the source, some people just think they are "a good person." 

Most experienced soulwinners will turn to Revelation 21:8;

*But the fearful, and unbelieving, and the abominable, and murderers, and whoremongers, and sorcerers, and idolaters, and all liars, shall have their part in the lake which burneth with fire and brimstone: which is the second death.* Here, we would highlight "all liars," and make the sincere appeal, "We have all told lies." This will often break the impasse. It is difficult to say to anyone with a straight face that you do not and never have told a lie. 

But for some who can't admit to being a liar, I have found **Proverbs 24:9** works well to push past that, because while we might justify our dishonesty to others as "harmless," or a "little white lie," everyone has stray thoughts and intrusive ideas that we are ashamed of. Covetous, lustful, wrathful and ultimately foolish. 

Some will pushback to any gospel presentation with scorn, those are an abomination. We shake the dust and move on.

<!-- post 17284 · 2026-03-14 19:19 · study -->

3 posts on **Proverbs 24**, and I haven't gotten halfway through the chapter. God's word is deep.

<!-- post 17285 · 2026-03-15 06:28 · study -->

Continuing the fatherly advice of the King, Solomon, to his son the prince,  **Proverbs 24:11-12**;

*If thou forbear to deliver them that are drawn unto death, and those that are ready to be slain; If thou sayest, Behold, we knew it not; doth not he that pondereth the heart consider it? and he that keepeth thy soul, doth not he know it? and shall not he render to every man according to his works?*

He says that failure to deliver someone marked for death by claiming ignorance to the circumstances, and potentially the injustice or false accusations that led to it, by not hearing the matter fully - that it is not an excuse. Solomon writes that we should consider our own soul, the Lord's mercy upon it, His judgment. That He will judge every man according to his works. 

This is echoed in the New Testament. 

**Romans 2:4-9**
*Or despisest thou the riches of his goodness and forbearance and longsuffering; not knowing that the goodness of God leadeth thee to repentance? But after thy hardness and impenitent heart treasurest up unto thyself wrath against the day of wrath and revelation of the righteous judgment of God; Who will render to every man according to his deeds: To them who by patient continuance in well doing seek for glory and honour and immortality, eternal life: But unto them that are contentious, and do not obey the truth, but obey unrighteousness, indignation and wrath, Tribulation and anguish, upon every soul of man that doeth evil, of the Jew first, and also of the Gentile*

**Hebrews 9:27**
*And as it is appointed unto men once to die, but after this the judgment.*

The wages of sin is death. We are all guilty. We will all be judged. But those in faith, who have believed on Christ, our sins not being imputed against our record, being justified by the righteousness of Jesus, our works, being built upon that foundation, are judged at the bema seat of Christ, to see what sort of works they are.

**1 Corinthians 3:12-15**
*Now if any man build upon this foundation gold, silver, precious stones, wood, hay, stubble; Every man's work shall be made manifest: for the day shall declare it, because it shall be revealed by fire; and the fire shall try every man's work of what sort it is. If any man's work abide which he hath built thereupon, he shall receive a reward. If any man's work shall be burned, he shall suffer loss: but he himself shall be saved; yet so as by fire.*

We are still saved, but, based on the criteria of God; the good will be rewarded and the vain and meaningless, destroyed. 

Meanwhile, the unbelieving are judged by their works at the Great White Throne. Their works held against them;

**Revelation 20:11-15**
*And I saw a great white throne, and him that sat on it, from whose face the earth and the heaven fled away; and there was found no place for them. And I saw the dead, small and great, stand before God; and the books were opened: and another book was opened, which is the book of life: and the dead were judged out of those things which were written in the books, according to their works. And the sea gave up the dead which were in it; and death and hell delivered up the dead which were in them: and they were judged every man according to their works. And death and hell were cast into the lake of fire. This is the second death. And whosoever was not found written in the book of life was cast into the lake of fire.*

With this perspective, which Solomon clearly understood, a ruler must make the effort to know what he is judging, especially in a capital crime where a life hangs in jeopardy. 

This warning applies to those that are princes by faith, in the Kingdom of God. To believers, every man we meet is appointed to death and Hell for their sins. They are drawn unto death, and ready to be slain. 

If we forbear to deliver them, to give them the gospel, we cannot say we didn't know. He that pondereth the heart and keepeth our soul, He knows it. And it will be counted a loss in the trying fire at the judgment seat of Christ.

<!-- post 17292 · 2026-03-17 05:24 · study -->

Making our way through what has become **Proverbs 24** week...the dense material of the "words of wisdom" given by Solomon to his son. 

 *Lay not wait, O wicked man, against the dwelling of the righteous; spoil not his resting place: For a just man falleth seven times, and riseth up again: but the wicked shall fall into mischief.*

The first half of verse 16 is often quoted, and encouraging. We may fall in our Christian walk, multiple times, but we rise up again and continue. God's mercies are new every morning. We slip and fall and stumble, but with the Lord, we will always have the means and chance to rise again. Even in death, we will rise, in Jesus. 

But there's an Important promise of wrath upon the wicked contained in this passage. The warning not to seek to rob the righteous, or to spoil his resting place; in his home, but also the grave. For what is buried in corruption will rise again to incorruption. The righteous rise, but the wicked shall fall. 

Knowing this, we are admonished against taking pleasure in their fall. We shouldn't be glad when they stumble, least God decide to turn His wrath away from him. Vengeance is His. God would that no man perish. In their fall is revealed His wrath, but it also is the opportunity to show His mercy and give the truth of the gospel, through us. 

We should therefore not worry and fret about what evil men do, not be envious of the wicked. God promises they will have no reward, they will be dealt with according to His perfect judgement. 

Too much of the online Christian community is caught up in fear mongering about the things the wicked do. About envying the political power or wealth they may have, or the respect that "normies" give them. They seemingly get away with awful things, while they threaten to persecute people who call themselves Christians. 

The way of the Lord is to not fret and to not doubt they will fall into mischief. God will repay. Our job is to be ready to provide every man an answer for our good conscience towards God and the hope we have in Christ.

<!-- post 17293 · 2026-03-17 05:43 · study -->

*My son, fear thou the Lord and the king: and meddle not with them that are given to change: For their calamity shall rise suddenly; and who knoweth the ruin of them both?*

The people who promote change as an end to itself, that we must "progress," to leave the old paths to strike out in the new, covet ideas of what they think could be, because they don't appreciate what is. 

In Christianity, this has resulted in more tragedy and departure from the Biblical truth found in the fear of the Lord. Taking His word as the final authority. 

Denominational control was a reaction to the fact that some churches fell into apostasy. This gave us the false religion of Catholicism. 

In recent times, the 19th and 20th century were plagued by modernism and changing of God's word to justify it. Even in the past few decades, churches that had resisted change and kept the right gospel, began to compromise with the world to "keep up with the times." Dropping standards, adding carnal appeals in music, technology, dress, "worship," to attract younger crowds. 

But the ruin of these changes comes suddenly. You can only go so far afield, and drift off the well-worn paths so far without finding yourself in sudden calamity. You can't move the landmarks of your fathers' and not become lost.

<!-- post 17299 · 2026-03-18 06:10 · study -->

*These things also belong to the wise. It is not good to have respect of persons in judgment.* 

Final entry in the **Proverbs 24** study, and we are again reminded to show no partiality or preference in dealing with people, especially under our judgment. If we have to determine truth, decide someone's date, or even just how we interact with each other, don't be a respecter of persons. God isn't. The rich or the lords among us should be subject to the same treatment as the lowly and poor. 

Note that we are also given other things to judge a man with over station; the words they speak, are they wise or froward? Do they have a reputation for wisdom? Generosity? Are they diligent or sluggards? Do they give their parents honor or woe? Are they drunkards? Do they plot wickedness and sow to themselves iniquity? Have you heard the matter fully, before witnesses? Is there mercy that can be shown? 

These are the factors we are to consider, but not favoritism that causes us to overlook right judgment. Not by appearances, as Jesus said. But right judgment. 

And for the judge, there is a warning;

*He that saith unto the wicked, Thou are righteous; him shall the people curse, nations shall abhor him: But to them that rebuke him shall be delight, and a good blessing shall come upon them.*

**Isaiah 5:20**
*Woe unto them that call evil good, and good evil; that put darkness for light, and light for darkness; that put bitter for sweet, and sweet for bitter*!

If you pervert justice and promote the wicked and cast your lot with the rich man over the widow or fatherless, if you justify the works of mischief, woe unto you. It is a blessing to call it straight and to rebuke that which is evil, and a curse to give cover to evil.

## Proverbs 25  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-20

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17301

<!-- post 17301 · 2026-03-20 05:21 · scripture -->

**Proverbs 25**
*These are also proverbs of Solomon, which the men of Hezekiah king of Judah copied out.*

*2 It is the glory of God to conceal a thing: but the honour of kings is to search out a matter.*

*3 The heaven for height, and the earth for depth, and the heart of kings is unsearchable.*

*4 Take away the dross from the silver, and there shall come forth a vessel for the finer.*

*5 Take away the wicked from before the king, and his throne shall be established in righteousness.*

*6 Put not forth thyself in the presence of the king, and stand not in the place of great men:*

*7 For better it is that it be said unto thee, Come up hither; than that thou shouldest be put lower in the presence of the prince whom thine eyes have seen.*

*8 Go not forth hastily to strive, lest thou know not what to do in the end thereof, when thy neighbour hath put thee to shame.*

*9 Debate thy cause with thy neighbour himself; and discover not a secret to another:*

*10 Lest he that heareth it put thee to shame, and thine infamy turn not away.*

*11 A word fitly spoken is like apples of gold in pictures of silver.*

*12 As an earring of gold, and an ornament of fine gold, so is a wise reprover upon an obedient ear.*

*13 As the cold of snow in the time of harvest, so is a faithful messenger to them that send him: for he refresheth the soul of his masters.*

*14 Whoso boasteth himself of a false gift is like clouds and wind without rain.*

*15 By long forbearing is a prince persuaded, and a soft tongue breaketh the bone.*

*16 Hast thou found honey? eat so much as is sufficient for thee, lest thou be filled therewith, and vomit it.*

*17 Withdraw thy foot from thy neighbour's house; lest he be weary of thee, and so hate thee.*

*18 A man that beareth false witness against his neighbour is a maul, and a sword, and a sharp arrow.*

*19 Confidence in an unfaithful man in time of trouble is like a broken tooth, and a foot out of joint.*

*20 As he that taketh away a garment in cold weather, and as vinegar upon nitre, so is he that singeth songs to an heavy heart.*

*21 If thine enemy be hungry, give him bread to eat; and if he be thirsty, give him water to drink:*

*22 For thou shalt heap coals of fire upon his head, and the Lord shall reward thee.*

*23 The north wind driveth away rain: so doth an angry countenance a backbiting tongue.*

*24 It is better to dwell in the corner of the housetop, than with a brawling woman and in a wide house.*

*25 As cold waters to a thirsty soul, so is good news from a far country.*

*26 A righteous man falling down before the wicked is as a troubled fountain, and a corrupt spring.*

*27 It is not good to eat much honey: so for men to search their own glory is not glory.*

*28 He that hath no rule over his own spirit is like a city that is broken down, and without walls.*

<!-- post 17302 · 2026-03-20 05:45 · study -->

**Proverbs 25** introduces a section (chapters 25–29) of Solomon’s proverbs that were compiled by King Hezekiah's officials, approximately 250–270 years after Solomon's reign. 

Hezekiah, the son of the wicked king, Ahaz, began a revival and reformation, tearing down altars and idols. In the first month of his reign, Hezekiah opened and repaired the doors of the House of the Lord. He commanded the Levites to "sanctify" themselves and carry *forth the filthiness out of the holy place*. He restored the observance of Passover. He brought order to the Levites, and proclaimed tithes and offerings from the people.

And in his zeal, the young king commanded scribes to write these sayings of Solomon, that they be preserved and observed. 

They begin with the honor of kings to search out the hidden things of God. 

**1 Corinthians 2:10**
*But God hath revealed them unto us by his Spirit: for the Spirit searcheth all things, yea, the deep things of God.*

As I have previously stated, my opinion is that the wisdom for princes and kings is for the saved, as co-heirs with Christ. Even the least in the Kingdom of Heaven is a prince, who will judge with Him in the millennium. We will judge angels. The study of the word is like unto princes under tutors, until the day of adoption. The resurrection where we come into our power as glorified sons of God. 

**Galatians 4**
*Now I say, That the heir, as long as he is a child, differeth nothing from a servant, though he be lord of all; But is under tutors and governors until the time appointed of the father*.

The context here is the law bringing man to understand the need for a savior, but the application mirrors the process of a prince coming into his own by instruction, until the determined time of the King. 

As Paul writes in **Romans 8:23** ...*even we ourselves groan within ourselves, waiting for the adoption, to wit, the redemption of our body.* The day of redemption, the resurrection, is the day of our adoption; the time of the giving of the inheritance, to rule and reign with Jesus. 

The Greek word Paul uses for 'adoption,' *huiothesia*, carries a heavier legal meaning than our modern idea of adoption. In that context, it wasn't about bringing an outsider into a family; it was the formal ceremony, the "placing as a son," where an heir reached the age of maturity, the father publicly recognized them as a legal adult, granting them the full authority, a signet ring, and power to manage the estate, or the lands appointed them. 

We become sons of God when we are born again, but notice the adoption is at the resurrection. At the day of redemption. 

So, when we see proverbs about kings or princes, about judgement and mercy, who to be influenced by, who to seek counsel from...it is all instructions from THE King, God our Father, to His children, who will inherit the Kingdom.

<!-- post 17303 · 2026-03-20 06:06 · study -->

Continuing in the chapter; if the deeper things of God are the glory of kings to search out, then the heart of the king is as high as Heaven and as deep as the earth; unsearchable to the common man. 

The Spirit that brings those who seek it, understanding, being absent in the natural man (who receiveth not the spiritual things of God) cannot fathom the heart of the king. 

This process, walking in the Spirit, seeking wisdom and the richness of God's judgment, produces a refinement; the fire of God removes the dross, the impurities in a man, and we come forth a finer vessel, pure silver, fit for holy use. 

While we have the legal inheritance to rule, as sons and co-heirs with Christ, we gain the character needed to rule, to judge righteous judgment, by this refinement. Our God is a consuming fire and all that burns away is dross. 

We are meant to be fitted as vessels of honor, and so the instructions for Godly living, His wisdom given by inspiration to Solomon, here written by order of Hezekiah, is designed to act as a refining fire. To see things the way God sees things. To walk His path, to be in the way of righteousness, following after Christ Jesus.

## Proverbs 26  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-26

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17322

<!-- post 17322 · 2026-03-26 05:26 · scripture -->

**Proverbs 26**
*As snow in summer, and as rain in harvest, so honour is not seemly for a fool.*

*2 As the bird by wandering, as the swallow by flying, so the curse causeless shall not come.*

*3 A whip for the horse, a bridle for the ass, and a rod for the fool's back.*

*4 Answer not a fool according to his folly, lest thou also be like unto him.*

*5 Answer a fool according to his folly, lest he be wise in his own conceit.*

*6 He that sendeth a message by the hand of a fool cutteth off the feet, and drinketh damage.*

*7 The legs of the lame are not equal: so is a parable in the mouth of fools.*

*8 As he that bindeth a stone in a sling, so is he that giveth honour to a fool.*

*9 As a thorn goeth up into the hand of a drunkard, so is a parable in the mouths of fools.*

*10 The great God that formed all things both rewardeth the fool, and rewardeth transgressors.*

*11 As a dog returneth to his vomit, so a fool returneth to his folly.*

*12 Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit? there is more hope of a fool than of him.*

*13 The slothful man saith, There is a lion in the way; a lion is in the streets.*

*14 As the door turneth upon his hinges, so doth the slothful upon his bed.*

*15 The slothful hideth his hand in his bosom; it grieveth him to bring it again to his mouth.*

*16 The sluggard is wiser in his own conceit than seven men that can render a reason.*

*17 He that passeth by, and meddleth with strife belonging not to him, is like one that taketh a dog by the ears.*

*18 As a mad man who casteth firebrands, arrows, and death,*

*19 So is the man that deceiveth his neighbour, and saith, Am not I in sport?*

*20 Where no wood is, there the fire goeth out: so where there is no talebearer, the strife ceaseth.*

*21 As coals are to burning coals, and wood to fire; so is a contentious man to kindle strife.*

*22 The words of a talebearer are as wounds, and they go down into the innermost parts of the belly.*

*23 Burning lips and a wicked heart are like a potsherd covered with silver dross.*

*24 He that hateth dissembleth with his lips, and layeth up deceit within him;*

*25 When he speaketh fair, believe him not: for there are seven abominations in his heart.*

*26 Whose hatred is covered by deceit, his wickedness shall be shewed before the whole congregation.*

*27 Whoso diggeth a pit shall fall therein: and he that rolleth a stone, it will return upon him.*

*28 A lying tongue hateth those that are afflicted by it; and a flattering mouth worketh ruin.*

<!-- post 17323 · 2026-03-26 06:03 · study -->

**Proverbs 26** continues the collection of Solomon's sayings, prepared for King Hezekiah, the 12th generation descendant of the wisest man ever to live. These sayings return to the more rapid fire style of earlier chapters, and focus heavily on the warnings against fools, liars, sluggards and talebearers. 

In order, vs 1 compares the honor given a fool to unseasonable weather. Snow in summer, rain at harvest. It should bother us as Christians when the foolish and froward get honor and praise by this world. We should see it as unseemly. It happens all too often, and vexes the heart. 

Curses without cause shall not come, curse not at all. It's vanity, generally speaking. The cause rarely rises to the level of the curse, and if it did, God will repay. Better to bless, and give it up to the Lord. 

The rod on the fools back serves the same purpose as a bridle, to direct the stubborn fool and keep him in line. Corporal punishment for foolish and malignant behavior is a good thing, and better at correction than jail, onerous fines and probation. Stripes serve a purpose, and it's cruelty that our society has abandoned this corrective in the name of progress.

Vs 4-5 bring a seeming contradiction;

*Answer not a fool according to his folly, lest thou also be like unto him. Answer a fool according to his folly, lest he be wise in his own conceit.*

The understanding here is that when a fool is arguing foolishness, debating their false premises, bickering back and forth makes you as much a fool, no matter how correct you are.

But to not answer a fool in his folly leaves him *wise in his own conceit*. The instruction then is to rebuke, not argue. To correct with authority (from scripture especially), and, as we've discussed, shake the dust. A **Titus 3:10** situation. 

To be "wise in your own conceits" is to be dug in or convinced of your false or nonsensical beliefs. It could be some weird doctrine that they've heard and taken to heart, repeating it as fact. It could be the conclusion of their own ignorance applying twisted logic. It could be lies and rationalizations they use to deceive themselves. 

It often is an identity they've adopted and the presuppositions and shibboleths that come with it, something we see in people taken in by special boy theology. They are constantly looking to provoke arguments and to get people into endless debates, that go no where. 

Verse 12 explains precisely why; *Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit? there is more hope of a fool than of him.*

Whatever the conceit may be, the fools are entrenched in them. And so, *after the first and second admonition reject.* You've better hope convincing a fool of the truth than changing their mind.

Using a fool as a messenger is foolish. It will undermine and damage what you sought to accomplish.

<!-- post 17324 · 2026-03-26 06:07 · study -->

Just as the lame struggle to walk, the fool will misuse parables to their own purposes, drawing wrong conclusions and clouding understanding, rather than gaining wisdom. 

While God uses parables throughout the Bible, Jesus teaching almost exclusively in parable to the hard-hearted Jews, so many have been used to create false doctrine. Proper application of parables should lay primarily on any explanation given in the text, (Jesus would often expound to the Apostles their meaning) but for those without clear teaching, the context should then be drawn by comparing scripture with scripture. No doctrine should stand alone on a parable. 

The most obvious example is the false doctrine of "Abraham's bosom,' taken from **Luke 16's** tale of the rich man and Lazarus. Jesus is using their example to convey a message of urgency to the Jews, to repent and believe the gospel before its too late. To say that the prophets and Moses gave witness to His day, harken to them and believe. That those who don't are Hell-bound, no matter how rich they are. That those with dull ears and blind eyes will not believe, even if one were to raise from the dead. 

But that the faithful, even the afflicted and poor in this world, who call upon the name of the Lord, are carried by angels into Heaven, honored and seated with Abraham, the father of faith. To be comforted forever. 

The esigesis and natural man logic required to turn this simple (and I believe, true) story into a doctrine that says that there is a place in Hell that's actually a compartment containing paradise, called "Abraham's bosom," and that that's where Old Testament saints went, is absurd. 

The embrace of Abraham is specifically to show how even the lowly beggar, covered in sores, whose life was misery and rejection, someone lower than the dogs without the rich man's gate, rejected by the respecters of persons Jesus is addressing, is given an honor that Jews would be envious of. To sit down with Abraham, in the paradise that is God's heavenly kingdom. 

As vs 9 says; *As a thorn goeth up into the hand of a drunkard, so is a parable in the mouths of fools.*

<!-- post 17325 · 2026-03-27 05:18 · study -->

The binding of a stone in a sling, trapping it there in its pocket, makes the weapon useless, and so it is equally vain to give honor to a fool. 

Sometimes, we try and build up a foolish person by encouraging them when they do right. Unfortunately, this generally puffs them up, and enables their wrongdoing by making them feel justified. The correction of a fool often fails, as we've seen in other passages, how less then their praise?

God, the Creator of all things, Who is blessed forever, amen, rewards the fool, and transgressors. They get what is coming to them, and His rebuke and reproach is far greater than anything we do.

*As a dog returneth to his vomit, so a fool returneth to his folly.* 

Peter quotes this proverb, and applies it to the false teachers, crept in. The apostates who hear the gospel preached and know the holy commandment to believe, yet fall away back to the rudiments of the world, their later end worse than before. 

**2 Peter 2:22**
*But it is happened unto them according to the true proverb, The dog is turned to his own vomit again; and the sow that was washed to her wallowing in the mire.*

The apostasy leads to reprobation. As **Hebrews 6** explains, those that have tasted of the truth, and the power of the Spirit, yet reject it, holding the truth in unrighteousness, become hardened. Unable to come to the knowledge of the truth. So they return to their unrighteousness as a dog to their vomit.

<!-- post 17326 · 2026-03-27 05:25 · study -->

*Seest thou a man wise in his own conceit? there is more hope of a fool than of him.*

The fool, wise in their own conceit, is the "special boy." Convinced that they know something to be true, despite clear scripture and common reason, context and consistent application across the text, they are immovable from their opinions. 

Their opinions are their own self-identity. They can no more consider they're wrong than deny their own name, because it would destroy the things that make up the very concept of who they are.

The more esoteric or counter to the plain reading, the better, because it allows them to feel superior to those that "just don't understand" their doctrine. They have carefully curated proof texts, and circular arguments and unfalsifiable theories. 

Again, this is why we shake the dust, and are told to reject them.

<!-- post 17327 · 2026-03-27 05:31 · study -->

The sluggard and slothful are next, and their ways mocked again. Their lame excuses to avoid work, their excessive sleeping habits, their self-destructive laziness. They're convinced that their rationalizations and justifications for refusing work cannot be reasoned or debated with. They simply don't care. 

It cannot be that the fault lies with them, and so it must be you being unreasonable. 

If you've ever had to deal with a sluggard, you know this tendency and how fruitless attempts to shame, cajole or rebuke them are. They only respond to starvation, and many even then cannot bring themselves to work.

<!-- post 17329 · 2026-03-28 08:31 · study -->

The last section of **Proverbs 26** deals with strife, and those that cause it with their lying tongue. 

*He that passeth by, and meddleth with strife belonging not to him, is like one that taketh a dog by the ears*.

Getting involved in other people's fights, whether it's taking up a cause between two parties in your church, between neighbors, or total strangers, is a foolish thing to do. The warning here leans into the uncontrollable danger, likening it to a dog turning and rending you. 

But it also applies to chatroom drama and Facebook fights. It happens online all the time, where the physical stakes are low, and the busybody tendencies are not inhibited by potential violence. 

Stay out of contentions that don't involve you. If we are to bless our own enemies and turn the other cheek, then what business is it of ours to join in another's vengeance? 

And, while it is blessed to be a peacemaker, it isn't arrived at by stepping in between brawlers or meddling in their strife. 

Better to counsel with soft words to the parties separately, than join in as a third member of an ongoing fight. You really don't want to be the reason two warring factions unite out of spite to go after you for your meddling. 

This should be applied in context to ruling a kingdom as well. When Jehosaphat, King of the Southern Kingdom of Judah joined the wicked Ahab of Israel in his war with Syria, we see in **2 Chronicles 19**; *Jehu the son of Hanani the seer went out to meet him, and said to king Jehoshaphat, Shouldest thou help the ungodly, and love them that hate the Lord? therefore is wrath upon thee from before the Lord.*

A godly nation should not join the enemies of Christ in war against their enemies. And Christians shouldn't cheer when their leaders yoke with unbelieving countries to make war. 

If two nations of those who hate God choose to go to war, you can pray for God's will to be done, or that His judgement brings peace. You can be indifferent to the outcome, and leave it to God. But don't meddle in their strife, even by egging it on.

<!-- post 17330 · 2026-03-28 08:59 · study -->

Next, we see that it is like a mad man randomly firing arrows, flaming arrows and sowing death to be a neighbor who lies to his neighbor for sport. To be "funny." To rile up a reaction through lies. 

The spirit behind it is the same. Seeking mischief to stir up strife is wicked as Hell. 

Finally, vs 20-22 and vs 23-28 deal with stirring up strife by being a talebearer and a liar. Strife caused by that little member, the tongue, that has such great power. 

**James 3:5-6**
*Even so the tongue is a little member, and boasteth great things. Behold, how great a matter a little fire kindleth! And the tongue is a fire, a world of iniquity: so is the tongue among our members, that it defileth the whole body, and setteth on fire the course of nature; and it is set on fire of hell.*

Talebearing and spreading rumors acts as kindling, as coals, keeping a fire going or sparking new fires. 

Gossiping to stir contention, to bring about trouble where there is none, causing wounds of the heart, is *like a potsherd covered with silver dross.* A shard of a broken pot, with the impurities and refined waste of silver. Worthless and wicked. 

Haters will lie and build up deceit within them. Paul warns Timothy of this kind of man;

**1 Timothy 6:4-5** 
*He is proud, knowing nothing, but doting about questions and strifes of words, whereof cometh envy, strife, railings, evil surmisings, Perverse disputings of men of corrupt minds, and destitute of the truth, supposing that gain is godliness: from such withdraw thyself.*

Evil surmising is the act of harboring unproven, malicious suspicions, assuming the worst motives about others without evidence. It is a destructive, divisive attitude that often produces pride, strife, and envy. They take innocent actions of someone and inventing malicious intent, spreading rumors to others to poison them against the hater's target of ire. 

Someone filled with hate can typically be characterized as having an unhealthy obsession with accusations that ruin fellowship, and sow discord among the brethren. Vs 25 specifically links discord and the other 6 things God hates, the 7 abominations from **Proverbs 6:16-19**, to these hate filled liars, flatterers, and talebearing deceivers;

*These six things doth the Lord hate: yea, seven are an abomination unto him: A proud look, a lying tongue, and hands that shed innocent blood, An heart that deviseth wicked imaginations, feet that be swift in running to mischief, A false witness that speaketh lies, and he that soweth discord among brethren.*

Don't be one, obviously, but also don't fall victim to one by listening to their talebearing. Their evil will be exposed and shown the whole congregation. The pit they dig to trap others will trap them. The stone they roll to crush their targets will return unto them. Their lying tongue and flattering mouth works ruin.

## Proverbs 27  ·  Primary study  ·  2026-03-30

Source: https://t.me/HouseOfWews2/17339

<!-- post 17339 · 2026-03-30 05:33 · scripture -->

**Proverbs 27**
*Boast not thyself of to morrow; for thou knowest not what a day may bring forth.*

*2 Let another man praise thee, and not thine own mouth; a stranger, and not thine own lips.*

*3 A stone is heavy, and the sand weighty; but a fool's wrath is heavier than them both.*

*4 Wrath is cruel, and anger is outrageous; but who is able to stand before envy?*

*5 Open rebuke is better than secret love.*

*6 Faithful are the wounds of a friend; but the kisses of an enemy are deceitful.*

*7 The full soul loatheth an honeycomb; but to the hungry soul every bitter thing is sweet.*

*8 As a bird that wandereth from her nest, so is a man that wandereth from his place.*

*9 Ointment and perfume rejoice the heart: so doth the sweetness of a man's friend by hearty counsel.*

*10 Thine own friend, and thy father's friend, forsake not; neither go into thy brother's house in the day of thy calamity: for better is a neighbour that is near than a brother far off.*

*11 My son, be wise, and make my heart glad, that I may answer him that reproacheth me.*

*12 A prudent man foreseeth the evil, and hideth himself; but the simple pass on, and are punished.*

*13 Take his garment that is surety for a stranger, and take a pledge of him for a strange woman.*

*14 He that blesseth his friend with a loud voice, rising early in the morning, it shall be counted a curse to him.*

*15 A continual dropping in a very rainy day and a contentious woman are alike.*

*16 Whosoever hideth her hideth the wind, and the ointment of his right hand, which bewrayeth itself.*

*17 Iron sharpeneth iron; so a man sharpeneth the countenance of his friend.*

*18 Whoso keepeth the fig tree shall eat the fruit thereof: so he that waiteth on his master shall be honoured.*

*19 As in water face answereth to face, so the heart of man to man.*

*20 Hell and destruction are never full; so the eyes of man are never satisfied.*

*21 As the fining pot for silver, and the furnace for gold; so is a man to his praise.*

*22 Though thou shouldest bray a fool in a mortar among wheat with a pestle, yet will not his foolishness depart from him.*

*23 Be thou diligent to know the state of thy flocks, and look well to thy herds.*

*24 For riches are not for ever: and doth the crown endure to every generation?*

*25 The hay appeareth, and the tender grass sheweth itself, and herbs of the mountains are gathered.*

*26 The lambs are for thy clothing, and the goats are the price of the field.*

*27 And thou shalt have goats' milk enough for thy food, for the food of thy household, and for the maintenance for thy maidens.*

<!-- post 17340 · 2026-03-30 06:07 · study -->

The record of Solomon's wisdom, as recorded by King Hezekiah's scribes, continues in **Proverbs 27**.

The litany of sayings found here offers some new insights, retelling of others and repetition of yet more, all for our edification. 

The chapter begins with the understanding that we should not assume what the future brings. We are prisoners of the moment, and our breath is in God's hands. Tomorrow isn't promised. 

We primarily use this Biblical understanding to stress the urgency of the gospel when preaching to the lost. That getting your account settled on the way cannot wait. Because, as the Bible says in **2 Corinthians 6:2** ...*I have heard thee in a time accepted, and in the day of salvation have I succoured thee: behold, now is the accepted time; behold, now is the day of salvation.*

If you haven't trusted on Christ as your only means of salvation, given by the grace of the Father, now is the time. 

If you have received that precious gift, get to work doing the will of God and winning souls. **Romans 13:11** ..*.now it is high time to awake out of sleep: for now is our salvation nearer than when we believed*

But even in general terms, boasting about what you will do tomorrow is an empty promise when you don't know what lies in store.

**James 4:14-15**
*Whereas ye know not what shall be on the morrow. For what is your life? It is even a vapour, that appeareth for a little time, and then vanisheth away. For that ye ought to say, If the Lord will, we shall live, and do this, or that.*

God willing, it shall come to pass, but better to deal with today, redeem the time and accomplish what may be done now, than seek glory for what you've yet to do. And even then, boast not at all;

*Let another man praise thee, and not thine own mouth; a stranger, and not thine own lips.*

It is in the nature of finite man to boast on the morrow while we procrastinate difficult things. To justify our not doing anything now, because of how wonderful our plans are for then. Getting lazy. Saying "If it's God's will" as an excuse. 

But what then? Should we not plan for the things that may come? To just wait on the Lord for all things? Not at all. Verse 12;

*A prudent man foreseeth the evil, and hideth himself; but the simple pass on, and are punished.*

Some, rather than foolishly passing on, or boasting on what they may do, get so caught up in worry for tomorrow that they are paralyzed today. Jesus teaches us to avoid that as well; 

**Matthew 6:34**
*Take therefore no thought for the morrow: for the morrow shall take thought for the things of itself. Sufficient unto the day is the evil thereof.*

We should recognize that the future exists, and the evil thereof, and plan accordingly. Being diligent in the works of today, is being prudent for tomorrow. 

*Whoso keepeth the fig tree shall eat the fruit thereof: so he that waiteth on his master shall be honoured.*

This is the correct balance, to see the future as an opportunity that's built in the work of the present, guarding against evil, while knowing the harvest will come. The reward is waiting. The honor and glory will come. Whether serving God and the spiritual works as a laborer for Christ, or just in your daily life in taking care of those things God has put before us. 

Achieve balance, seek Him first, be prudent and plan, without worry, don't out off till tomorrow what can be done today. Do not seek your own glory and honor, or boast about what you plan to do, but work diligently knowing you will earn the praise of others, even God, Amen.

<!-- post 17345 · 2026-03-31 05:57 · study -->

Following along in chapter **27:13**, we are again reminded to avoid guaranteeing the debt of someone unknown or unreliable, to take their coat or a pledge as collateral. This of course, is always wise, or to swear not at all for a stranger. 

Verse 14, don't be an annoying neighbor. Even a good thing, such as blessing a friend, is counted a curse when done at an inappropriate or inopportune time. 

*A continual dropping in a very rainy day and a contentious woman are alike. Whosoever hideth her hideth the wind, and the ointment of his right hand, which bewrayeth itself.*

It is clear that Solomon had issue with contentious women, and the Spirit confirms this plight. As annoying as a continuous dropping or rain, impossible to hide. Like holding back the wind or a handful of oil, her nagging and arguing is impossible to contain. 

Don't be that woman. Don't be that henpecked husband. 

Next, an often quoted verse, Iron sharpeneth iron. We in the Christian life should cultivate other Christians as friends. And as friends, we can help hone one another, as sharpening a blade, to improve each other. 

**Hebrews 10:23-24**
*Let us hold fast the profession of our faith without wavering; (for he is faithful that promised;) And let us consider one another to provoke unto love and to good works*

A friend can sharpen the countenance of his friend, then to, as the reflection of your face in water revealed your appearance, so too is your heart revealed when you deal man to man. In other words, our friends can be a mirror into our own heart. 

When we have the opportunity to see ourselves as we are, don't ignore it or forget it. It's a means to improve. To sharpen our iron. The way we see ourselves without reflection, is often tainted and false. Built up by our own rationalizing and self-deception. But our heart is made known to us by our interactions with others. It is also reflected back at us in reading Scripture. 

 **James 1:23-25**
*For if any be a hearer of the word, and not a doer, he is like unto a man beholding his natural face in a glass: For he beholdeth himself, and goeth his way, and straightway forgetteth what manner of man he was. But whoso looketh into the perfect law of liberty, and continueth therein, he being not a forgetful hearer, but a doer of the work, this man shall be blessed in his deed.*

We should relish the opportunity to see ourselves as we really are, unashamed because we are walking God's path. Being a doer or the word, not a hearer only. That when we see our heart, man to man we are not ashamed. And that others look at us and see the love of Christ.

<!-- post 17346 · 2026-03-31 06:09 · study -->

*Though thou shouldest bray a fool in a mortar among wheat with a pestle, yet will not his foolishness depart from him.*

This saying carries another reminder that fools are locked in to their foolishness, that they are wise in their own conceit and even stripes won't make them listen. Not even grinding them in a mortar and pestle could remove their foolishness. 

Finally, as in earlier parts of the chapter, is the lesson in the diligence of now, the favoring of low time preference and having a long view. 

Tend your flocks. Look well to thy herds. Manage the resources and wealth you have, and don't be wasteful or careless. Riches are not forever. The crown does not endure to every generation. These things can be squandered and lost by poor management, slothfulness and foolishness. 

God provides the tender grass and herbs of the mountain. The food for your flock. The means of your substance. The ability to make a living depends on His grace and generosity, and our diligence. 

Keeping this in mind, He promises that we shall have what is sufficient to care for our family. 

If we take care of our worldly wealth, and manage and work it, taking advantage of all the Lord provides, how much more the treasure we store up in Heaven? 

The Lord provides us His word to feed the flock, as the grass and herb. We all have opportunities to serve Him and reach the lost, to tend to the flocks He has given us. It may a mother over her child. It may be as a teacher to young men. It may be as a Pastor over a church. But our diligence to know the state of them, and look well over our herd, will benefit ourselves and our family in the long run.
